<a id="stmt-lem-distinguished-meridians"></a>
<div id="lem:distinguished-meridians" class="lemma">

**Lemma 15**. *Every meridian around a component of $D_*$ has trivial image under $\rho_0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-distinguished-meridians"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-distinguished-meridians</summary>

*Proof.* A main-component meridian is represented by the circuit along the fixed exterior section, so it is killed by <a href="#eq:rho-zero" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rho-zero">[eq:rho-zero]</a>.

For a tail use the blowup chart $w=uv$. A meridian at a sufficiently large fixed slope $v=c$ is represented downstairs by $$u=\varepsilon e^{i\theta},\qquad
 w=c\varepsilon e^{i\theta},\qquad 0\le\theta\le2\pi.$$ Choose $|c|$ larger than the two branch slopes and then choose $\varepsilon$ small. Trivialize the pair motion by an isotopy supported inside the circle containing the two marks but not this meridian. In this trivialization the loop is the base circuit followed by one full pair-boundary loop, up to conjugacy and the choice of multiplication convention. The base circuit is killed by the section splitting; the pair boundary is killed by the pinching map. Both factors therefore have trivial image. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### The remaining boundary relations

There are finitely many components of $D$ outside $D_*$. Choose based meridians $\mu_1,\ldots,\mu_b$ for them, and choose lifts $\widetilde\mu_i\in L$ of $\rho_0(\mu_i)\in Q_0$. These lifts exist since $L\to Q_0$ is surjective. Choose an integer $e$ so large that <a id="eq:boundary-cost"></a>$$\label{eq:boundary-cost}
 b\,t^{2^e}<\frac1{100},$$ and impose the additional relators $\widetilde\mu_i^{2^e}$. This is the high-power method underlying Golod’s construction (Golod 1964) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[Golod1964](#ref-Golod1964)], applied here to the finitely many boundary elements. Each added relator has degree at least $2^e$ by <a href="#eq:degree-rules" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:degree-rules">[eq:degree-rules]</a>. Denote the resulting pro-2 quotient by $Q$.

<a id="stmt-prop-infinite-quotient"></a>
<div id="prop:infinite-quotient" class="proposition">

**Proposition 16**. *The group $Q$ is infinite. The homomorphism $$\rho:\pi_1(U)\longrightarrow Q$$ induced by $\rho_0$ has dense image on every punctured fiber. Each component meridian of $D$ has finite image, and every component meridian of $D_*$ has trivial image.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-infinite-quotient"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-infinite-quotient</summary>

*Proof.* Only infinitude remains to prove. The generators of $L$ have polynomial $P(t)$ and the involution relators have cost at most $tG(t)$, where [prop:involution](#prop:involution) gives $$P(t)=(1+t)G(t),\qquad
 G(t)=3t(1+t)(1+t^2)^2.$$ At the rational value <a href="#eq:t-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:t-choice">[eq:t-choice]</a>, $t^2>3/50$, so $$G(t)>
 \frac{3\cdot49\cdot249\cdot53^2}{200^2\cdot50^2}
 =\frac{102817827}{100000000}
 >\frac{257}{250}.$$ The complete weighted expression in [lem:weighted-gs](#lem:weighted-gs) is therefore less than $$1-P(t)+tG(t)+\frac1{100}+\frac1{100}
 =1-G(t)+\frac1{50}
 <-\frac1{125}<0.$$ The infinite monodromy-relator family is finite in each bounded degree and has convergent cost by <a href="#eq:monodromy-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:monodromy-cost">[eq:monodromy-cost]</a>. The remaining families are finite. All hypotheses of [lem:weighted-gs](#lem:weighted-gs) hold, proving that $Q$ is infinite. Passage to the quotient preserves all earlier equalities and the density of each fiber image. ◻

</details>

Notice that $e$ is chosen after the finite cover and compactification. Thus their possibly large number of boundary components introduces no circular parameter choice. The surface construction now uses only the finite local images and the infinite fiber image recorded in [prop:infinite-quotient](#prop:infinite-quotient).

<a id="sec:surface"></a>
### The projective surface and its rational fiber

We now turn the quotient of [prop:infinite-quotient](#prop:infinite-quotient) into a fundamental-group image on a smooth projective surface. The construction must retain the distinguished fiber: its rational components will lift compactly, while the fundamental group of their union will have infinite image.

Recall the inputs. The complement $U=Y\setminus D$ lies in a smooth connected projective surface $Y$, the boundary $D$ has simple normal crossings, and $$\rho:\pi_1(U)\longrightarrow Q$$ has dense image in an infinite pro-$2$ group. Its restriction to a punctured smooth fiber also has dense image. Every component meridian of $D$ has finite image, and the meridians of the distinguished fiber $D_*$ have trivial image. This fiber consists of an unmarked main sphere and $24$ tails, each meeting the main sphere once and carrying two horizontal markings.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### A finite cover that kills all compactification meridians

At a crossing, two commuting meridians may have relations that neither cyclic subgroup detects separately. We therefore choose a finite quotient that detects the entire local group at every boundary point.

<a id="stmt-lem-local-detection"></a>
<div id="lem:local-detection" class="lemma">

**Lemma 17** (Finite local detection and descent). *Let $Y$ be a smooth connected projective complex surface, let $D$ be a simple-normal-crossings divisor, and put $U=Y\setminus D$. Suppose $\rho:\pi_1(U)\to Q$ has dense image in a profinite group and sends every component meridian of $D$ to an element of finite order. There is a finite quotient $Q\twoheadrightarrow P_f$ with the following properties.*

1.  *The quotient is injective on the image under $\rho$ of every sufficiently small local boundary-complement group.*

2.  *The connected finite cover $\pi:U'\to U$ defined by $\ker(\pi_1(U)\to P_f)$ extends to a projective morphism $p:X\to Y$, where $X$ is smooth and connected and $D_X=X\setminus U'$ is a simple-normal-crossings divisor.*

<a id="eq:rho-descent"></a>
3.  *Writing $i:U'\hookrightarrow X$ for inclusion, there is a homomorphism $\rho_X:\pi_1(X)\to Q$ such that $$\rho_X\circ i_* = \rho\circ\pi_*.
        \label{eq:rho-descent}$$*

*Moreover, the resolution used to construct $X$ can be chosen to preserve every open subset of the normalization on which the surface is smooth and its reduced boundary has simple normal crossings.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-local-detection"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-local-detection</summary>

*Proof.* We first find a finite quotient detecting all local boundary images, then compactify and resolve the corresponding cover, and finally check that the original quotient map kills every new boundary meridian.

Choose a small coordinate polydisk $V$ about a point of $D$. Its boundary complement is $$V\setminus D\simeq(\Delta^*)^r\times\Delta^{2-r},
  \qquad r\in\{1,2\}.$$ After choosing an access path in $U$, let $A_V\subset Q$ be the image of its fundamental group. This group is generated by $r$ commuting finite-order elements, so it is finite. Only finitely many such images occur up to conjugacy: there is one meridian type on the smooth part of each boundary component and one local group at each crossing. The smooth part of an irreducible component, with its finitely many crossings removed, is connected; moving the access path along it conjugates the local group.

<a id="eq:local-detection"></a>
For every nonidentity element in representatives of these finite groups, choose an open normal subgroup of $Q$ that does not contain it. Their finite intersection $N$ is open and normal. It satisfies $$N\cap gA_Vg^{-1}=\{1\}
  \qquad(g\in Q)
  \label{eq:local-detection}$$ for every local group. Set $P_f=Q/N$. Density makes the map $\pi_1(U)\to P_f$ surjective, so its kernel defines a connected finite Galois cover $\pi:U'\to U$.

The cover has the complex structure obtained by lifting local charts. It is algebraic and finite *étale* by the Riemann existence theorem for complex varieties (Grothendieck et al. 2003, Exposé XII, Théorème 5.1) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[SGA1](#ref-SGA1)]. This theorem applies to the nonproper surface $U$. Normalize $Y$ in the function field of $U'$ and denote the resulting morphism by $n:\overline Y\to Y$. It is finite: complex varieties are Nagata, so finiteness of relative normalization applies to the finite-type morphism $U'\to Y$ (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tag 03GR) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[StacksProject](#ref-StacksProject)]. Its restriction over $U$ is $U'$, because the latter is already normal. Finite morphisms are projective, so $\overline Y$ is projective (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tag 0B3I) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[StacksProject](#ref-StacksProject)].

Resolve $\overline Y$ by projective blowups, leaving its smooth locus unchanged; strong resolution in characteristic zero has this prescribed-open property (Encinas and Hauser 2002, 822–23) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[EncinasHauser2002](#ref-EncinasHauser2002)]. Next resolve the reduced boundary on the resulting smooth surface. For curves on a smooth surface this can be done by point blowups: resolve the singular component germs, decrease tangency intersection multiplicities, and separate crossings of three or more branches (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tag 0BIC and its proof) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[StacksProject](#ref-StacksProject)]. Choose these centers only where the boundary is not already simple normal crossings. Thus this additional operation preserves every open subset on which the pair was already smooth with simple normal crossings. We obtain $$X\xrightarrow{\ r\ }\overline Y
    \xrightarrow{\ n\ }Y,
  \qquad p=n\circ r,$$ with $X$ smooth and projective and with $D_X$ a simple-normal-crossings divisor. The dense open set $U'$ is unchanged, so $X$ is connected.

We prove the factorization, including the meridians of resolution exceptional curves. The inclusion $i$ is surjective on fundamental groups: a loop can be perturbed off a real-codimension-two divisor. Its kernel is normally generated by the component meridians of $D_X$. Indeed, perturb a null-homotopy disk relative to its boundary so that it avoids the finitely many crossings of $D_X$ and meets its smooth part transversely. Deleting small disks around the finitely many intersection points expresses its boundary loop as a product of conjugates of meridians and their inverses.

Let $E$ be any component of $D_X$, choose a smooth point $x\in E$ away from the other components, and take a small transverse disk $T$ through $x$ with $T\setminus\{x\}\subset U'$. Choose a boundary polydisk $V$ about $p(x)$ downstairs. By shrinking $T$, we may assume $p(T)\subset V$. Consequently the projection of a small meridian in $T\setminus\{x\}$ lies in $V\setminus D$. After including its access path, its $Q$-image belongs to a conjugate of $A_V$. This argument applies also when $E$ is exceptional and is contracted to a boundary point.

The projected meridian has trivial image in $P_f$, since its lift is a closed loop in $U'$. Its $Q$-image therefore lies in both $N$ and a conjugate of $A_V$, and is trivial by <a href="#eq:local-detection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-detection">[eq:local-detection]</a>. All generators of $\ker i_*$ are thus killed by $\rho\circ\pi_*$. This proves <a href="#eq:rho-descent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rho-descent">[eq:rho-descent]</a>. ◻

</details>

Apply [lem:local-detection](#lem:local-detection) to the map supplied by [prop:infinite-quotient](#prop:infinite-quotient). Retain its notation $P_f,N,U',\overline Y,X,p,\rho_X$. We next verify that the normalization is already smooth along the distinguished fiber, so that the resolution can preserve it.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### The distinguished fiber remains a union of spheres

<a id="stmt-lem-rational-special-fiber"></a>
<div id="lem:rational-special-fiber" class="lemma">

**Lemma 18**. *Let $h:X\to C'$ be the composite of $p$ and $Y\to C'$. The resolution can be chosen so that the reduced fiber $$Z=h^{-1}(c_*)_{\mathrm{red}}$$ has only smooth rational irreducible components and ordinary transverse crossings.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-rational-special-fiber"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-rational-special-fiber</summary>

*Proof.* Every vertical meridian along $D_*$ is trivial in $Q$, and hence in $P_f$, by [lem:distinguished-meridians](#lem:distinguished-meridians). We describe the normalization locally at every kind of point of $D_*$. The restriction of the Galois cover to a boundary-complement polydisk is classified by its local meridian homomorphism to $P_f$; its connected components correspond to cosets of the local image.

At a smooth unmarked point of $D_*$, the sole meridian is trivial. Every connected local cover therefore extends as a copy of the polydisk. At a node between the main component and a tail, both vertical meridians are trivial, and the same assertion holds.

At a horizontal–vertical intersection choose coordinates $(u,v)$ with the vertical curve $u=0$ and the horizontal marking $v=0$. The $u$-meridian is trivial. If the image of the $v$-meridian has order $e$, the kernel of the local map $\mathbb Z^2\to P_f$ is $\mathbb Z\times e\mathbb Z$. Each connected local cover thus has the finite normal extension <a id="eq:distinguished-local-cover"></a>$$(u,w)\longmapsto (u,v)=(u,w^e).
  \label{eq:distinguished-local-cover}$$ This extension is smooth, and its reduced boundary is $uw=0$. Analytification preserves finiteness and normality (Grothendieck et al. 2003, Exposé XII, Propositions 2.1(vi) and 3.2(vi)) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[SGA1](#ref-SGA1)]. Thus the local extensions just described agree with $\overline Y$ by uniqueness of finite normal extension (Grothendieck et al. 2003, Exposé XII, Proposition 5.3) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[SGA1](#ref-SGA1)]. The entire preimage of $D_*$ lies in the smooth simple-normal-crossings locus of the normalized pair. The resolution in [lem:local-detection](#lem:local-detection) can be the identity on this open neighborhood.

Consider now an irreducible component above the main sphere. Its map to that sphere is unramified, including at the attaching nodes, and there are no horizontal markings on the main component. It is therefore a connected unramified cover of $\mathbb P^1$, hence a sphere mapping with degree one. A component above a tail can branch only at its two horizontal markings, and is unramified at the attaching node. Removing those two points downstairs and all their preimages upstairs gives a connected finite cover of $\mathbb C^*$. Its covering subgroup is $d\mathbb Z\subset\mathbb Z$. Thus it is the covering $z\mapsto z^d$ of $\mathbb C^*$, whose smooth compactification is $\mathbb P^1$.

There are no additional components from resolution over $D_*$. The local models show that the components just identified are smooth and meet only in ordinary transverse crossings. This proves the assertion about $Z$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### Infinite image on the compact fiber

Although $D_*$ is a tree of spheres, a degree-$d$ component above one of its tails meets $d$ distinct degree-one lifts of the main sphere: the attaching node has $d$ distinct preimages. The incidence graph upstairs therefore need not be a tree. For a connected nodal union of simply connected components, van Kampen identifies its fundamental group with that of its dual graph, which has one vertex for each component and one edge for each node. We now prove that some connected component of $Z$ has infinite fundamental-group image in $X$.

<a id="stmt-lem-infinite-special-image"></a>
<div id="lem:infinite-special-image" class="lemma">

**Lemma 19**. *Let $Z=h^{-1}(c_*)_{\mathrm{red}}$ be the rational nodal fiber of [lem:rational-special-fiber](#lem:rational-special-fiber). Some connected component $Z_0$ of $Z$ satisfies $$\bigl|\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\bigl(\pi_1(Z_0)\longrightarrow\pi_1(X)\bigr)\bigr|
  =\infty.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-infinite-special-image"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-infinite-special-image</summary>

*Proof.* Let $F_s\subset U$ be a punctured smooth fiber, with $s\in B'$, and choose access paths when comparing its group with $\pi_1(U)$. Its image under $\rho$ is dense in $Q$ by [prop:infinite-quotient](#prop:infinite-quotient). In particular its image in $P_f$ is all of $P_f$, so its inverse image $F'_s\subset U'$ is connected. Under the covering inclusion, its group is the preimage of $N$ in $\pi_1(F_s)$. Thus the image of $\pi_1(F'_s)$ in $Q$ is $$\rho\bigl(\pi_1(F_s)\bigr)\cap N.$$ It is dense in $N$, because $N$ is open and the fiber image is dense in $Q$. The group $N$ is infinite: a finite open subgroup would make its finite-index overgroup $Q$ finite. Consequently the image of $\pi_1(F'_s)$ in $Q$ is infinite. By <a href="#eq:rho-descent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rho-descent">[eq:rho-descent]</a>, its image in $\pi_1(X)$ is infinite as well.

We transfer this conclusion to $Z$ using an explicit deformation-retract neighborhood. Regard $X$ as a compact real analytic manifold and $Z$ as a closed semianalytic subset. This pair admits a compatible finite triangulation (Łojasiewicz 1964, sec. 3, Theorem 2, p. 464) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[Lojasiewicz1964](#ref-Lojasiewicz1964)]. Write it as a finite simplicial pair $(\mathcal K,\mathcal A)$, and barycentrically subdivide so that $\mathcal A$ is a full subcomplex: any simplex whose vertices belong to $\mathcal A$ belongs to $\mathcal A$. For a point of $|\mathcal K|$, let $s_{\mathcal A}$ be the sum of its barycentric coordinates at vertices of $\mathcal A$. On the open neighborhood $$V=\{s_{\mathcal A}>0\}$$ retain those coordinates, divide them by $s_{\mathcal A}$, and set all other coordinates to zero. Fullness ensures that the result belongs to $|\mathcal A|$. The straight-line homotopy inside each simplex gives a strong deformation retraction $V\to Z$. In particular the inclusion of $V$ into $X$ is homotopic to a map through $Z$.

Properness of $h$ supplies the needed containment of nearby fibers. The set $h(X\setminus V)$ is closed in $C'$ and does not contain $c_*$. Hence every whole fiber over a sufficiently small neighborhood of $c_*$ lies in $V$. Choose $s\in B'$ in this neighborhood. Its connected punctured lifted fiber $F'_s$ lies in one connected component of $V$; the deformation retraction takes that component into a connected component $Z_0$ of $Z$. Therefore its inclusion-induced homomorphism to $\pi_1(X)$ factors, up to a change-of-basepoint conjugation, through $\pi_1(Z_0)$. Since the former has infinite image, so does the latter. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-infinite-special-image-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-infinite-special-image</summary>

*Proof of [thm:main](#thm:main).* The construction above gives a smooth connected projective surface $X$. By [lem:rational-special-fiber](#lem:rational-special-fiber), [lem:infinite-special-image](#lem:infinite-special-image), it contains a connected nodal curve $Z_0$ with smooth rational irreducible components and infinite image in $\pi_1(X)$.

now applies to $(X,Z_0)$. In the simply connected universal cover $\widetilde X$, every connected component $W$ above $Z_0$ is a closed noncompact locally finite union of compact rational curves. For every $w\in W$, the holomorphic hull of $\{w\}$ contains $W$ and is noncompact. This proves all the assertions of [thm:main](#thm:main). ◻

</details>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The actual sphere group $Π$ maps to every pro-$2$ quotient $R$ of $L$ on which sign acts trivially. The triangle records $θp = λΦ|_{K}$. The map $p$ is surjective and $Φ|_{K}$ has dense image. Monodromy is compared on $K$ and $Π$ before it is passed to $R$.",
      "figure_id": "fig:group-maps",
      "pdf_page": 12,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:group-maps",
          "exact_quote": "The actual sphere group $\\Pi$ maps to every pro-$2$ quotient\n$R$ of $L$ on which sign acts trivially. The triangle records\n$\\theta p=\\lambda\\Phi|_K$. The map $p$ is surjective and $\\Phi|_K$\nhas dense image. Monodromy is compared on $K$ and ",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[baseline=(current bounding box.center),>=Stealth]\n \\node (K) at (0,1) {$K$};\n \\node (L) at (2.8,1) {$L$};\n \\node (R) at (5.6,1) {$R$};\n \\node (Pi) at (0,-0.4) {$\\Pi$};\n \\draw[->] (K) -- node[above] {$\\Phi|_K$} (L);\n \\draw[->] (L) -- node[above] {$\\lambda$} (R);\n \\draw[->] (K) -- node[left] {$p$} (Pi);\n \\draw[->] (Pi) -- node[below right] {$\\theta$} (R);\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Commutative diagram with four vertices: $K$ at the top left, $L$ at the top center, $R$ at the top right, and $\\Pi$ at the bottom left directly below $K$. A horizontal arrow points from $K$ to $L$ labeled $\\Phi|_K$, a horizontal arrow points from $L$ to $R$ labeled $\\lambda$, a vertical downward arrow points from $K$ to $\\Pi$ labeled $p$, and a diagonal arrow points from $\\Pi$ upward and rightward to $R$ labeled $\\theta$."
    },
    {
      "caption": "The distinguished fiber $D_{*}$ on $Y$, after the base change of index two and one blowup at each collision. Each of the $24$ rational tails has two markings (solid dots) and one node on the main component (open circles). The middle $20$ tails are omitted. The cover constructed in is unramified at the nodes and can branch only at the markings. Its inverse image may have a different incidence graph.",
      "figure_id": "fig:distinguished-fiber",
      "pdf_page": 19,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:distinguished-fiber",
          "exact_quote": "The distinguished fiber $D_*$ on $Y$, after the base change of index two and\none blowup at each collision. Each of the $24$ rational tails has two\nmarkings (solid dots) and one node on the main component (open circles).\nThe middle $20$ tail",
          "source_object": "fig:0002"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1.12cm,y=0.85cm]\n \\draw[thick] (0,0) -- (8,0);\n \\foreach \\x/\\lab in {0.8/1,2.2/2,5.8/23,7.2/24} {\n  \\draw[thick] (\\x,-0.12) -- (\\x,2);\n  \\draw[fill=white] (\\x,0) circle (2.5pt);\n  \\fill (\\x,0.7) circle (2pt);\n  \\fill (\\x,1.35) circle (2pt);\n  \\node[above] at (\\x,2) {tail $\\lab$};\n }\n \\node at (4,1.05) {$\\cdots$};\n \\node[below] at (4,-0.3) {main $\\PP^1$: no markings};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A diagram of the distinguished fiber $D_*$. A horizontal line segment at the bottom represents the base component, labeled below as \"main $\\mathbb{P}^1$: no markings\". Four vertical line segments rise upward from the horizontal line, labeled at the top as \"tail 1\", \"tail 2\", \"tail 23\", and \"tail 24\", with three horizontal dots \"$\\cdot\\ \\cdot\\ \\cdot$\" between tail 2 and tail 23. Each vertical tail attaches to the horizontal line at an open circle representing a node, and each tail features two solid dots vertically spaced along the segment representing marked sections."
    }
  ],
